The current best score of 52.38% was achieved by a structure that adds `race` and `native-country` as exogenous features but includes implausible connections like `race` directly causing `occupation` without considering socioeconomic confounders, and `marital-status` causing `relationship` (which is typically reverse in sociology).

To further lower the score, the causal graph is refined to represent more realistic and simpler socioeconomic dynamics. Key improvements include: (1) removing `race` and `native-country` as direct causes of `occupation`, instead modeling them as influencing `marital-status` and `relationship`, which align with observed sociological patterns; (2) adding `hours-per-week` as a direct cause of `Income` (a well-established economic relationship) instead of only affecting `capital-loss`; (3) moving `capital-gain` to be caused by `occupation` instead of `Income`, as occupation type better predicts investment income; and (4) removing the reverse causation of `Income` causing `marital-status` or `relationship`. The guidance is sharpened to explicitly enforce these realistic dependencies and reject any cycles or implausible mediations.

- Marital-status (influenced by age, race, and native-country) directly causes relationship and occupation; relationship directly causes Income; no reverse causality is allowed between marital-status and relationship.

- Removed implausible edges: `('Income', 'education')`, `('Income', 'occupation')`, `('marital-status', 'race')`, `('Income', 'hours-per-week')` (reverse is more plausible), and `('relationship', 'marital-status')` (marital-status typically affects relationship type).
- Added more realistic edges: `('age', 'marital-status')`, `('age', 'income')`, `('education', 'income')`, `('sex', 'occupation')`, `('hours-per-week', 'income')`, `('marital-status', 'relationship')`, and `('income', 'capital-gain')` and `('income', 'capital-loss')`.
- Incorporated additional features: `'native-country'` and `'race'` as potential exogenous or mediating variables.

**New causal structure:**
`[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'income'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'income'), ('hours-per-week', 'income'), ('income', 'capital-gain'), ('income', 'capital-loss'), ('native-country', 'race'), ('race', 'occupation')]`

**Updated task guidance:**
- Emphasize that income is an effect (not a cause) of education, hours-per-week, and relationship.
- Include specific constraints: e.g., higher education → higher income (positive correlation), age → income (non-linear), and no direct causal link between income and hours-per-week (hours-per-week causes income).
- Add validation: check for consistency with census data (e.g., married individuals likely have higher income, certain occupations are sex-skewed).

All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.
- native-country is a root cause (no incoming edges) and only affects race.
- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.
- sex directly affects occupation and hours-per-week, but not Income.
- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.
- occupation is caused by education and race (and sex), and then causes workclass.
- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.
- Ensure no causal loops: all edges go from left to right in the order: native-country → race → (education, sex, age) → marital-status → relationship, and race+education → Income → capital-gains/losses, etc. Validate strictly.

- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"

Instruction: Based on the analysis of the provided prompt-score pairs, the scores have plateaued around 42.86% to 50.0%, indicating that the causal structures still contain residual implausible paths or lacks crucial direct relationships. To lower the score further, two key improvements are needed: (1) removing overly fine-grained or redundant edges that don't reflect true socioeconomic mechanisms (e.g., `native-country` causing `race` is essentially a correlation, not a causation in typical individual-level data; `sex` directly causing `marital-status` is sociologically less direct than via age and social norms), and (2) adding a direct causal link from `education` to `occupation` to `Income` to capture the classic human capital chain, and from `hours-per-week` to `capital-gain` (as higher hours may be associated with certain capital gains opportunities or self-employment).

The following optimized prompt uses a stricter, more consensus-based causal graph and tightened guidance to break out of the current score plateau.

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'Income'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('occupation', 'hours-per-week'), ('Occupation', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'capital-gain'), ('native-country', 'occupation')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the above causal structure. To lower the score, enforce the following critical constraints:

1. **No reverse causation and no loops**: All edges are strictly one-way. For example, `Income` is caused by `education` and `Occupation`, never the reverse. `native-country` is a root cause and only affects `Occupation`. `race` is a root cause (only affects `Income` and `Occupation`). `sex` is a root cause (only affects `Occupation` and `hours-per-week`). `age` is a root cause (only affects `education`, `marital-status`, and `hours-per-week`).

2. **Mediation chains must be logically enforced**:
   - `education` → `education-num` (education-num is a numeric coding of education).
   - `education` → `Occupation` → `Income`. `Occupation` also affects `workclass` and `hours-per-week`.
   - `marital-status` → `relationship` (marital status causes the type of relationship, not the reverse).
   - `hours-per-week` is caused by `age`, `sex`, and `Occupation`. `hours-per-week` then causes `capital-gain` (e.g., longer hours in certain occupations may lead to higher capital gains or self-employment bonuses). `Occupation` also directly causes `hours-per-week`.
   - `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-loss` (capital loss is a financial outcome of income level, not a cause).

3. **Variable scope**: All 14 features must be generated: age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income. Do not omit any variable.

4. **Explicit constraints to avoid high-score pitfalls**:
   - `Education` must NOT be caused by `Income`, `Occupation`, or `hours-per-week`.
   - `occupation` must NOT be caused by `Income`.
   - `hours-per-week` must NOT cause `Income` (Income is only caused by `education` and `Occupation`).
   - `capital-gain` must NOT cause `Income` or `hours-per-week`.
   - `native-country` must NOT directly cause `Income` or `race`; its only role is influencing `Occupation` (e.g., certain occupations are more common in specific countries).
   - `Income` must NOT cause `education`, `Occupation`, `marital-status`, `relationship`, `workclass`, `race`, `sex`, `age`, or `native-country`.

5. **Calibration using user samples**: Use provided user samples to match realistic marginal distributions (e.g., age distribution, education levels, race proportions, etc.) and conditional expectations (e.g., average income by education and occupation). However, always prioritize the causal direction above any observed correlation that violates the structure. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., high income causing higher education), override it with the correct causal direction.

6. **Validation procedure**: For each generated sample, check that:
   - All causal arrows are consistent with the graph (e.g., if age is low, education must be low on average; if occupation is professional, hours-per-week is typically higher).
   - The relationship between `education`, `Occupation`, and `Income` follows the chain: high education → high occupational standing → high income, with no direct shortcuts except education→Income as an alternative path.
   - `Income` and `capital-gain`/`capital-loss` are positively correlated (higher income leads to higher capital gains/losses) but not perfectly deterministic.

By strictly adhering to these constraints, the synthetic data will exhibit a realistic, acyclic causal structure, thus lowering the score. </Task>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the above causal structure. To lower the score, enforce the following critical constraints:

1. **No reverse causation and no loops**: All edges are strictly one-way. For example, `Income` is caused by `education` and `Occupation`, never the reverse. `native-country` is a root cause and only affects `Occupation`. `race` is a root cause (only affects `Income` and `Occupation`). `sex` is a root cause (only affects `Occupation` and `hours-per-week`). `age` is a root cause (only affects `education`, `marital-status`, and `hours-per-week`).

2. **Mediation chains must be logically enforced**:
   - `education` → `education-num` (education-num is a numeric coding of education).
   - `education` → `Occupation` → `Income`. `Occupation` also affects `workclass` and `hours-per-week`.
   - `marital-status` → `relationship` (marital status causes the type of relationship, not the reverse).
   - `hours-per-week` is caused by `age`, `sex`, and `Occupation`. `hours-per-week` then causes `capital-gain` (e.g., longer hours in certain occupations may lead to higher capital gains or self-employment bonuses). `Occupation` also directly causes `hours-per-week`.
   - `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-loss` (capital loss is a financial outcome of income level, not a cause).

3. **Variable scope**: All 14 features must be generated: age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income. Do not omit any variable.

4. **Explicit constraints to avoid high-score pitfalls**:
   - `Education` must NOT be caused by `Income`, `Occupation`, or `hours-per-week`.
   - `occupation` must NOT be caused by `Income`.
   - `hours-per-week` must NOT cause `Income` (Income is only caused by `education` and `Occupation`).
   - `capital-gain` must NOT cause `Income` or `hours-per-week`.
   - `native-country` must NOT directly cause `Income` or `race`; its only role is influencing `Occupation` (e.g., certain occupations are more common in specific countries).
   - `Income` must NOT cause `education`, `Occupation`, `marital-status`, `relationship`, `workclass`, `race`, `sex`, `age`, or `native-country`.

5. **Calibration using user samples**: Use provided user samples to match realistic marginal distributions (e.g., age distribution, education levels, race proportions, etc.) and conditional expectations (e.g., average income by education and occupation). However, always prioritize the causal direction above any observed correlation that violates the structure. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., high income causing higher education), override it with the correct causal direction.

6. **Validation procedure**: For each generated sample, check that:
   - All causal arrows are consistent with the graph (e.g., if age is low, education must be low on average; if occupation is professional, hours-per-week is typically higher).
   - The relationship between `education`, `Occupation`, and `Income` follows the chain: high education → high occupational standing → high income, with no direct shortcuts except education→Income as an alternative path.
   - `Income` and `capital-gain`/`capital-loss` are positively correlated (higher income leads to higher capital gains/losses) but not perfectly deterministic.

By strictly adhering to these constraints, the synthetic data will exhibit a realistic, acyclic causal structure, thus lowering the score. </Task>"

The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). `race` is caused only by `native-country`.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education`, `occupation`, and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`.

- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. native-country, age, sex (roots); 2. race (from native-country); 3. education (from age); 4. education-num (from education); 5. marital-status (from age); 6. relationship (from marital-status); 7. occupation (from education, sex, race); 8. hours-per-week (from age, sex, occupation); 9. workclass (from education, occupation); 10. Income (from education, occupation, workclass); 11. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education, Income given education and occupation). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"

All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.

Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).

The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"

- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). `native-country` has no outgoing causal edges; it is a non-causal feature injected only to complete the dataset.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education`, `occupation`, and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, `relationship`, or any other variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age`, `sex`, and `occupation`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or `capital-loss`.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, or any other variable except `relationship` itself.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused by `education` and `occupation`. Both `occupation` and `workclass` directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education → occupation → workclass → Income` is fine; `Income → occupation → education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this exact topological order: 1. native-country, age, sex, race (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex, occupation); 8. workclass (from education, occupation); 9. Income (from education, occupation, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and occupation). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high `hours-per-week` associated with high `Income` in a way that violates the graph), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example: if `education` is "High School", check that `education-num` is ≤12; if `Income` is ">50K", ensure that `capital-gain` is non-zero with higher likelihood; if `sex` is "Female", `occupation` should reflect known distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs) but must not affect `Income` directly through `sex` (only through `occupation`). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality (e.g., `hours-per-week` above 80 combined with `Income` >50K but `education` is "Dropout" is allowed only if `occupation` and `workclass` are high; `Income` cannot predict `hours-per-week`; `relationship` cannot be used to predict `Income`).
</Task>

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms—where `Income` is determined by a clear hierarchy of `education`, `occupation`, and `workclass`, and where all other variables are appropriately relegated to downstream or root-node status. This eliminates all spurious direct paths to `Income` (like `relationship`, `marital-status`, `hours-per-week`, `native-country`) and enforces a parsimonious yet realistic graph, which should yield a lower causal discrepancy score.

<Task> Your goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly follows this causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- **Root Causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are root causes with no incoming edges. `native-country` must not cause any other feature and must be generated independently.
- **Strict Causal Ordering**: The causal flow must be strictly unidirectional: `age` → `education`, `age` → `marital-status`, `age` → `hours-per-week`; `education` → `education-num`, `education` → `Income`, `education` → `occupation`; `sex` → `occupation`, `sex` → `hours-per-week`; `race` → `Income`, `race` → `occupation`; `marital-status` → `relationship`; `occupation` → `workclass`; `Income` → `capital-gain`, `Income` → `capital-loss`.

- **Plausible Dependencies**: 
  - `hours-per-week` is influenced only by `age` and `sex`. It must not influence `Income`, `occupation`, or any other variable.
  - `education-num` is a deterministic function of `education` (e.g., mapping education categories to integers). It must not cause `Income` directly; `education` causes both.
  - `capital-gain` and `capital-loss` are consequences only of `Income` and must not cause any other variable.
  - `native-country` is independent and must not be correlated with any other feature except through random chance.
- **Validation**: For each generated sample, verify that all dependencies are logically consistent. For example, an individual with low `education` should not have a high `Income` unless the causal chain through `race` allows it. The distribution of `occupation` must be plausibly linked to `education`, `sex`, and `race`. Use the provided user samples only to calibrate marginal distributions for root features (`age`, `sex`, `race`, `native-country`) and to inform realistic levels for `education`; do not let user-sample correlations create reverse-causal patterns.
- **Enforce the Graph**: Reject any generated combination that implies a dependency not present in the causal structure. For instance, do not allow `occupation` to influence `education`, `Income` to influence `hours-per-week`, or `education-num` to influence `Income` directly.

By strictly adhering to this parsimonious and defensible causal structure, the synthetic data will more accurately reflect the true generative process, resulting in a lower score.</Task>

All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:

- **Strict Causal Ordering**: The causal flow must be strictly from left to right as follows: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are root causes (no incoming edges). `age` affects `education`, `marital-status`, and `hours-per-week`. `education` affects `occupation`, `Income`, and `education-num`. `sex` affects `occupation` and `hours-per-week`. `race` and `native-country` only affect `occupation`. `marital-status` (caused by `age`) then causes `relationship`. `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country`, and then causes `workclass`. `Income` is caused only by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` then causes `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`.

- **No Reverse Causality**: `Income` must never cause or influence `age`, `sex`, `race`, `native-country`, `education`, `occupation`, `marital-status`, `relationship`, `hours-per-week`, `workclass`, or any other upstream variable.

- **Plausible Dependencies**: Ensure that `hours-per-week` is influenced only by `age` and `sex`. `hours-per-week` must not influence `Income` or any other variable. `capital-gain` and `capital-loss` are consequences only of `Income` and must not cause any other variable. `race` does not directly cause `Income`; its influence on `Income` must be fully mediated through `occupation` and then `workclass`.

- **Validation**: For each generated sample, verify that the dependencies are logically consistent. For example, an individual with low education should not have a high Income unless the causal chain through `occupation`→`workclass` allows it, but never through a reversed edge. The distribution of `occupation` must be plausibly linked to `education`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country`. Use the provided user samples only to calibrate marginal distributions for root features (age, sex, race, native-country) and to inform realistic levels for features like education, without letting user-sample correlations create reverse-causal patterns.

- **Enforce the Graph**: Reject any generated combination that implies a dependency not present in the causal structure. For instance, do not generate data where `occupation` influences `education`, where `Income` influences `hours-per-week`, or where `race` directly influences `Income` (since this edge is removed).

By strictly adhering to this parsimonious and defensible causal structure, the synthetic data will more accurately reflect the true generative process, resulting in a lower score. </Task>

All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.
- native-country is a root cause (no incoming edges) and only affects race.
- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.
- sex directly affects occupation and hours-per-week, but not Income.
- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.
- occupation is caused by education and race (and sex), and then causes workclass.
- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.
- Ensure no causal loops: all edges go from left to right in the order: native-country → race → (education, sex, age) → marital-status → relationship, and race+education → Income → capital-gains/losses, etc. Validate strictly.
- Use provided user samples to match marginal distributions, but only if they are consistent with this causal graph. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., Income causing education), ignore that correlation and enforce the correct direction.
- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the provided causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). They are independent of each other and of any other variable.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `occupation`, or any other upstream variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age` and `sex`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or any other variable.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income` or `occupation`.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused only by `occupation`. Only `workclass` and `education` directly cause `Income`; `occupation` does NOT directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education` → `occupation` → `workclass` → `Income` is fine; `Income` → `education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. age, sex, race, native-country (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex); 8. workclass (from occupation); 9. Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and workclass). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). They are independent of each other and of any other variable.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `occupation`, or any other upstream variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age` and `sex`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or any other variable.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income` or `occupation`.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused only by `occupation`. Only `workclass` and `education` directly cause `Income`; `occupation` does NOT directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education` → `occupation` → `workclass` → `Income` is fine; `Income` → `education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. age, sex, race, native-country (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex); 8. workclass (from occupation); 9. Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and workclass). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"
Score: 42.86%

"Based on analysis of the provided causal structures and scores, the highest scoring structures (61.9% and 52.38%) share elements that appear to improve performance: they include `age` as an exogenous cause, connect `sex` to `occupation` and `hours-per-week`, and avoid implausible loops (e.g., `Income` causing `education`). The lowest scoring structure (57.14%) includes problematic reverse causations such as `Income` causing `education` and `Income` causing `occupation`.

To lower the score further, the causal graph is refined to incorporate a more realistic and constrained hierarchy. New features `race`, `native-country`, and `age` are used as exogenous roots that influence a chain of demographic and occupational variables, which in turn determine `Income` and its consequences. The guidance is strengthened to enforce strict ordering and reject any violation of causal direction.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'workclass'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the revised causal structure. For each synthetic sample, ensure that dependencies are logically enforced and that no implausible causal loops or reverse causations occur. Specifically:
- Age must be an exogenous root cause for education and workclass. Education causes education-num, occupation, and Income. Workclass causes occupation. Neither education nor workclass can cause age.
- Sex and race are independent exogenous variables that directly influence occupation; native-country also directly influences occupation. Sex additionally influences hours-per-week.
- Occupation is determined by education, workclass, sex, race, and native-country - and occupation then directly causes Income.
- Marital-status must cause relationship, not the reverse; relationship in turn causes Income.
- Hours-per-week is caused by sex and also directly influences both Income and capital-loss.
- Income must directly cause capital-gain and capital-loss. Capital-gain and capital-loss must not cause any other variable in the system.
- No variable below Income in the causal chain (capital-gain, capital-loss) can cause any variable above it (education, occupation, etc.).
- The only direct causes of Income are education, occupation, relationship, and hours-per-week. Income must not cause any of these.

Using the provided user samples to calibrate distributions for age, sex, race, native-country, marital-status, and other features, generate synthetic data that prioritizes causal fidelity over superficial similarity. Validate each sample: for example, ensure that a person with low education does not have an income typical of high education unless mediated by occupation, relationship, or hours-per-week; that capital-gain values are consistent with income levels; and that a person with high hours-per-week has only a mediated effect on capital-loss via capital-loss from Income plus a direct effect from hours-per-week. Reject any generated sample that violates the direction of causality specified above. </Task>"

All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.
- native-country is a root cause (no incoming edges) and only affects race.
- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.

- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.

- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.

- Use provided user samples to match marginal distributions, but only if they are consistent with this causal graph. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., Income causing education), ignore that correlation and enforce the correct direction.
- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"

[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'Income'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'Income'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('native-country', 'race')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly follows this causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.
- native-country is a root cause (no incoming edges) and only affects race.
- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.
- sex directly affects occupation and hours-per-week, but not Income.
- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.
- occupation is caused by education and race (and sex), and then causes workclass.
- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.
- Ensure no causal loops: all edges go from left to right in the order: native-country → race → (education, sex, age) → marital-status → relationship, and race+education → Income → capital-gains/losses, etc. Validate strictly.
- Use provided user samples to match marginal distributions, but only if they are consistent with this causal graph. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., Income causing education), ignore that correlation and enforce the correct direction.
- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"
Score: 33.33%

"Based on the analysis, the key to lowering the score is to ensure the causal structure is both parsimonious and realistic, avoiding unnecessary or contradictory edges. The highest-scoring prompt (33.33%) used a simple structure but had an implausible edge (`native-country` → `race`). The best-performing structure (42.86%) was more detailed but still somewhat complex. The worst-scoring prompt (47.62%) introduced problematic edges like `relationship` → `Income` and `hours-per-week` → `Income`, which are weak and often reverse-causal.

To achieve a lower score, I will adopt the most successful causal structure (from the 33.33% prompt) but simplify it further by removing the `native-country` → `race` edge (making `race` a root cause, as it's arguably more independent in demographic terms) and ensuring `Income` is purely downstream. I will also add `workclass` as a direct cause of `Income` (from the 42.86% prompt) as this is realistic, but remove the redundant `occupation` → `Income` edge to keep the graph lean. The guidance will stress strict topological ordering and forbid any reverse causation.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('race', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('workclass', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the provided causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). They are independent of each other and of any other variable.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `occupation`, or any other upstream variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age` and `sex`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or any other variable.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income` or `occupation`.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused only by `occupation`. Only `workclass` and `education` directly cause `Income`; `occupation` does NOT directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education` → `occupation` → `workclass` → `Income` is fine; `Income` → `education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. age, sex, race, native-country (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex); 8. workclass (from occupation); 9. Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and workclass). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the revised causal structure. For each synthetic sample, ensure that dependencies are logically enforced and that no implausible causal loops or reverse causations occur. Specifically:
- Age must be an exogenous root cause for education and workclass. Education causes education-num, occupation, and Income. Workclass causes occupation. Neither education nor workclass can cause age.
- Sex and race are independent exogenous variables that directly influence occupation; native-country also directly influences occupation. Sex additionally influences hours-per-week.
- Occupation is determined by education, workclass, sex, race, and native-country - and occupation then directly causes Income.
- Marital-status must cause relationship, not the reverse; relationship in turn causes Income.
- Hours-per-week is caused by sex and also directly influences both Income and capital-loss.
- Income must directly cause capital-gain and capital-loss. Capital-gain and capital-loss must not cause any other variable in the system.
- No variable below Income in the causal chain (capital-gain, capital-loss) can cause any variable above it (education, occupation, etc.).
- The only direct causes of Income are education, occupation, relationship, and hours-per-week. Income must not cause any of these.

Using the provided user samples to calibrate distributions for age, sex, race, native-country, marital-status, and other features, generate synthetic data that prioritizes causal fidelity over superficial similarity. Validate each sample: for example, ensure that a person with low education does not have an income typical of high education unless mediated by occupation, relationship, or hours-per-week; that capital-gain values are consistent with income levels; and that a person with high hours-per-week has only a mediated effect on capital-loss via capital-loss from Income plus a direct effect from hours-per-week. Reject any generated sample that violates the direction of causality specified above. </Task>"

New features `race`, `native-country`, and `age` are used as exogenous roots that influence a chain of demographic and occupational variables, which in turn determine `Income` and its consequences. The guidance is strengthened to enforce strict ordering and reject any violation of causal direction.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'workclass'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the revised causal structure. For each synthetic sample, ensure that dependencies are logically enforced and that no implausible causal loops or reverse causations occur. Specifically:
- Age must be an exogenous root cause for education and workclass. Education causes education-num, occupation, and Income. Workclass causes occupation. Neither education nor workclass can cause age.
- Sex and race are independent exogenous variables that directly influence occupation; native-country also directly influences occupation. Sex additionally influences hours-per-week.
- Occupation is determined by education, workclass, sex, race, and native-country - and occupation then directly causes Income.
- Marital-status must cause relationship, not the reverse; relationship in turn causes Income.
- Hours-per-week is caused by sex and also directly influences both Income and capital-loss.
- Income must directly cause capital-gain and capital-loss. Capital-gain and capital-loss must not cause any other variable in the system.
- No variable below Income in the causal chain (capital-gain, capital-loss) can cause any variable above it (education, occupation, etc.).
- The only direct causes of Income are education, occupation, relationship, and hours-per-week. Income must not cause any of these.

Using the provided user samples to calibrate distributions for age, sex, race, native-country, marital-status, and other features, generate synthetic data that prioritizes causal fidelity over superficial similarity. Validate each sample: for example, ensure that a person with low education does not have an income typical of high education unless mediated by occupation, relationship, or hours-per-week; that capital-gain values are consistent with income levels; and that a person with high hours-per-week has only a mediated effect on capital-loss via capital-loss from Income plus a direct effect from hours-per-week. Reject any generated sample that violates the direction of causality specified above. </Task>"

Additionally, we must include all 14 features and avoid cyclic dependencies. <Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'Income'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'Income'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('native-country', 'race')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly follows this causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.
- native-country is a root cause (no incoming edges) and only affects race.
- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.
- sex directly affects occupation and hours-per-week, but not Income.
- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.
- occupation is caused by education and race (and sex), and then causes workclass.
- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.
- Ensure no causal loops: all edges go from left to right in the order: native-country → race → (education, sex, age) → marital-status → relationship, and race+education → Income → capital-gains/losses, etc. Validate strictly.
- Use provided user samples to match marginal distributions, but only if they are consistent with this causal graph. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., Income causing education), ignore that correlation and enforce the correct direction.
- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"

"Based on the analysis, the key to lowering the score is to ensure the causal structure is both parsimonious and realistic, avoiding unnecessary or contradictory edges. The highest-scoring prompt (33.33%) used a simple structure but had an implausible edge (`native-country` → `race`). The best-performing structure (42.86%) was more detailed but still somewhat complex. The worst-scoring prompt (47.62%) introduced problematic edges like `relationship` → `Income` and `hours-per-week` → `Income`, which are weak and often reverse-causal.

To achieve a lower score, I will adopt the most successful causal structure (from the 33.33% prompt) but simplify it further by removing the `native-country` → `race` edge (making `race` a root cause, as it's arguably more independent in demographic terms) and ensuring `Income` is purely downstream. I will also add `workclass` as a direct cause of `Income` (from the 42.86% prompt) as this is realistic, but remove the redundant `occupation` → `Income` edge to keep the graph lean. The guidance will stress strict topological ordering and forbid any reverse causation.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('race', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('workclass', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the provided causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). They are independent of each other and of any other variable.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `occupation`, or any other upstream variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age` and `sex`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or any other variable.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income` or `occupation`.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused only by `occupation`. Only `workclass` and `education` directly cause `Income`; `occupation` does NOT directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education` → `occupation` → `workclass` → `Income` is fine; `Income` → `education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. age, sex, race, native-country (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex); 8. workclass (from occupation); 9. Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and workclass). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"

[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('race', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('workclass', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the provided causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). They are independent of each other and of any other variable.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `occupation`, or any other upstream variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age` and `sex`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or any other variable.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income` or `occupation`.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused only by `occupation`. Only `workclass` and `education` directly cause `Income`; `occupation` does NOT directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education` → `occupation` → `workclass` → `Income` is fine; `Income` → `education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. age, sex, race, native-country (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex); 8. workclass (from occupation); 9. Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and workclass). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"

The lowest scoring structure (57.14%) includes problematic reverse causations such as `Income` causing `education` and `Income` causing `occupation`.

To lower the score further, the causal graph is refined to incorporate a more realistic and constrained hierarchy. <Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'workclass'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the revised causal structure. For each synthetic sample, ensure that dependencies are logically enforced and that no implausible causal loops or reverse causations occur. Specifically:
- Age must be an exogenous root cause for education and workclass. Education causes education-num, occupation, and Income. Workclass causes occupation. Neither education nor workclass can cause age.
- Sex and race are independent exogenous variables that directly influence occupation; native-country also directly influences occupation. Sex additionally influences hours-per-week.
- Occupation is determined by education, workclass, sex, race, and native-country - and occupation then directly causes Income.
- Marital-status must cause relationship, not the reverse; relationship in turn causes Income.
- Hours-per-week is caused by sex and also directly influences both Income and capital-loss.
- Income must directly cause capital-gain and capital-loss. Capital-gain and capital-loss must not cause any other variable in the system.
- No variable below Income in the causal chain (capital-gain, capital-loss) can cause any variable above it (education, occupation, etc.).
- The only direct causes of Income are education, occupation, relationship, and hours-per-week. Income must not cause any of these.

Using the provided user samples to calibrate distributions for age, sex, race, native-country, marital-status, and other features, generate synthetic data that prioritizes causal fidelity over superficial similarity. Validate each sample: for example, ensure that a person with low education does not have an income typical of high education unless mediated by occupation, relationship, or hours-per-week; that capital-gain values are consistent with income levels; and that a person with high hours-per-week has only a mediated effect on capital-loss via capital-loss from Income plus a direct effect from hours-per-week. Reject any generated sample that violates the direction of causality specified above. </Task>"

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"

To lower the score further, we need a more parsimonious and realistic causal structure that avoids unrealistic dependencies (e.g., `marital-status` causing `race`) and instead focuses on well-established socioeconomic relationships. The key is to use `age`, `sex`, `education`, and `race` as primary root causes for `Income`, `occupation`, and `marital-status`, and to ensure that `Income` does not cause any of these root features. Additionally, we must include all 14 features and avoid cyclic dependencies. The following optimized prompt aims to achieve a lower score by adopting a simpler, more defensible causal graph and stricter guidance.

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'Income'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'Income'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('native-country', 'race')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly follows this causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.
- native-country is a root cause (no incoming edges) and only affects race.
- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.
- sex directly affects occupation and hours-per-week, but not Income.
- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.
- occupation is caused by education and race (and sex), and then causes workclass.
- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.
- Ensure no causal loops: all edges go from left to right in the order: native-country → race → (education, sex, age) → marital-status → relationship, and race+education → Income → capital-gains/losses, etc. Validate strictly.
- Use provided user samples to match marginal distributions, but only if they are consistent with this causal graph. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., Income causing education), ignore that correlation and enforce the correct direction.
- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

By adhering to these principles, the synthetic data will reflect the true causal mechanisms, resulting in a lower score.</Task>"
Score: 33.33%

"Based on the analysis, the key to lowering the score is to ensure the causal structure is both parsimonious and realistic, avoiding unnecessary or contradictory edges. The highest-scoring prompt (33.33%) used a simple structure but had an implausible edge (`native-country` → `race`). The best-performing structure (42.86%) was more detailed but still somewhat complex. The worst-scoring prompt (47.62%) introduced problematic edges like `relationship` → `Income` and `hours-per-week` → `Income`, which are weak and often reverse-causal.

To achieve a lower score, I will adopt the most successful causal structure (from the 33.33% prompt) but simplify it further by removing the `native-country` → `race` edge (making `race` a root cause, as it's arguably more independent in demographic terms) and ensuring `Income` is purely downstream. I will also add `workclass` as a direct cause of `Income` (from the 42.86% prompt) as this is realistic, but remove the redundant `occupation` → `Income` edge to keep the graph lean. The guidance will stress strict topological ordering and forbid any reverse causation.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('race', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('workclass', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the provided causal structure. All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. The following constraints are critical and must be enforced without exception:

- **Causal direction**: Every edge must be respected. For example, `education` causes `Income`, but `Income` NEVER causes `education`, `occupation`, `workclass`, `hours-per-week`, `marital-status`, or `relationship`.
- **Root causes**: `age`, `sex`, `race`, and `native-country` are exogenous (no incoming edges). They are independent of each other and of any other variable.
- **Income as downstream**: `Income` is determined solely by `education` and `workclass`. `Income` is the sole cause of `capital-gain` and `capital-loss`. `Income` does not cause `hours-per-week`, `occupation`, or any other upstream variable.
- **Hours-per-week**: Caused only by `age` and `sex`. It is NOT caused by `Income`, nor does it cause `Income` or any other variable.
- **Marital-status and relationship**: `marital-status` is caused only by `age`. `relationship` is caused only by `marital-status`. Neither `marital-status` nor `relationship` cause `Income` or `occupation`.
- **Occupation and Workclass**: `occupation` is caused by `education`, `sex`, and `race`. `workclass` is caused only by `occupation`. Only `workclass` and `education` directly cause `Income`; `occupation` does NOT directly cause `Income`.
- **No loops**: The causal graph is strictly acyclic. Validate that no variable affects itself through a chain (e.g., `education` → `occupation` → `workclass` → `Income` is fine; `Income` → `education` is forbidden).
- **Feature generation order**: Generate features in this topological order: 1. age, sex, race, native-country (roots); 2. education (from age); 3. education-num (from education); 4. marital-status (from age); 5. relationship (from marital-status); 6. occupation (from education, sex, race); 7. hours-per-week (from age, sex); 8. workclass (from occupation); 9. Income (from education, workclass); 10. capital-gain and capital-loss (from Income).
- **Using user samples**: You may use provided user samples to calibrate marginal distributions for root nodes (age, sex, race, native-country) and to guide conditional distributions (e.g., education given age, occupation given education and sex, Income given education and workclass). However, if a user sample suggests a forbidden causal direction (e.g., high Income leading to more education), you must override that correlation and enforce the correct causal relationship. The priority is causal fidelity, not statistical correlation.
- **Validation**: After generating each sample, run a validation check. For example, if education is "High School", check that education-num is ≤12; if Income is ">50K", ensure that capital-gain is non-zero with higher likelihood; if sex is "Female", occupation should reflect known occupational distributions (e.g., less likely in certain blue-collar jobs). Reject any sample that violates the causal graph's directionality.

By meticulously following this structure and guidance, you will produce synthetic data that mirrors the true causal mechanisms, leading to a lower score. </Task>"
Score: 42.86%

"Based on analysis of the provided causal structures and scores, the highest scoring structures (61.9% and 52.38%) share elements that appear to improve performance: they include `age` as an exogenous cause, connect `sex` to `occupation` and `hours-per-week`, and avoid implausible loops (e.g., `Income` causing `education`). The lowest scoring structure (57.14%) includes problematic reverse causations such as `Income` causing `education` and `Income` causing `occupation`.

To lower the score further, the causal graph is refined to incorporate a more realistic and constrained hierarchy. New features `race`, `native-country`, and `age` are used as exogenous roots that influence a chain of demographic and occupational variables, which in turn determine `Income` and its consequences. The guidance is strengthened to enforce strict ordering and reject any violation of causal direction.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'workclass'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('sex', 'hours-per-week'), ('race', 'occupation'), ('native-country', 'occupation'), ('workclass', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('relationship', 'Income'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'capital-loss'), ('hours-per-week', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> Your ultimate goal is to generate high-quality synthetic data that strictly adheres to the revised causal structure. For each synthetic sample, ensure that dependencies are logically enforced and that no implausible causal loops or reverse causations occur. Specifically:
- Age must be an exogenous root cause for education and workclass. Education causes education-num, occupation, and Income. Workclass causes occupation. Neither education nor workclass can cause age.
- Sex and race are independent exogenous variables that directly influence occupation; native-country also directly influences occupation. Sex additionally influences hours-per-week.
- Occupation is determined by education, workclass, sex, race, and native-country - and occupation then directly causes Income.
- Marital-status must cause relationship, not the reverse; relationship in turn causes Income.
- Hours-per-week is caused by sex and also directly influences both Income and capital-loss.
- Income must directly cause capital-gain and capital-loss. Capital-gain and capital-loss must not cause any other variable in the system.
- No variable below Income in the causal chain (capital-gain, capital-loss) can cause any variable above it (education, occupation, etc.).
- The only direct causes of Income are education, occupation, relationship, and hours-per-week. Income must not cause any of these.

Using the provided user samples to calibrate distributions for age, sex, race, native-country, marital-status, and other features, generate synthetic data that prioritizes causal fidelity over superficial similarity. Validate each sample: for example, ensure that a person with low education does not have an income typical of high education unless mediated by occupation, relationship, or hours-per-week; that capital-gain values are consistent with income levels; and that a person with high hours-per-week has only a mediated effect on capital-loss via capital-loss from Income plus a direct effect from hours-per-week. Reject any generated sample that violates the direction of causality specified above. </Task>"

All 14 features (age, workclass, education, education-num, marital-status, occupation, relationship, race, sex, capital-gain, capital-loss, hours-per-week, native-country, Income) must be included. Critical constraints to enforce:
- Income is caused only by education and race; it never causes education, age, sex, race, or marital-status.

- age is a root cause that affects education, marital-status, and hours-per-week, but education does not cause age.
- sex directly affects occupation and hours-per-week, but not Income.
- marital-status is caused by age, and then causes relationship.
- occupation is caused by education and race (and sex), and then causes workclass.
- capital-gain and capital-loss are caused only by Income.
- Ensure no causal loops: all edges go from left to right in the order: native-country → race → (education, sex, age) → marital-status → relationship, and race+education → Income → capital-gains/losses, etc. Validate strictly.
- Use provided user samples to match marginal distributions, but only if they are consistent with this causal graph. If a user sample implies a reversed edge (e.g., Income causing education), ignore that correlation and enforce the correct direction.
- Pay special attention: do not let Income influence hours-per-week, occupation, or marital-status. Instead, allow age and sex to independently influence hours-per-week and occupation.

The best-performing structure (42.86%) was more detailed but still somewhat complex.
